>[!IMPORTANT]
> For brevity, I've skipped writing $\lceil\text{ceil}\rceil$ annotations around every equation in this post, but it's important to always round up to the nearest whole number in these calculations. Rounding down can result in subtle sizing errors that make your pattern a few stitches too large. 

## Framing The Pattern

The first step in choosing a good hoop and aida is ensuring that your pattern is square. If it's not already square-shaped, project it onto a square whose sides are of the length of the longer side of the pattern, then shift the pattern down (if it's wider than it is tall) or right (if it's taller than it is wide) by ${|w-h|}\over{2}$ pixels. We'll call this side length $n$. 

Next, create a circle whose diameter $d$ is equal to $n$. The circle should fit perfectly inside the bounds of the square, such that each of its sides are tangent to the circle at $n\over 2$ on the square's sides and at intervals of $90\degree$ (${1 \over 2}\pi\text{ rad}$) around the circle.

<figcaption>Fig.1 - A circle with diameter <i>n</i> inside a square with side length <i>n</i>. Each of the square's sides is tangent to the circle.</figcaption>
</figure>

If this frames the pattern well, great! If not, we can use some maths to fit it better. 

To make the square fit perfectly within the circle, we need to apply a scale of $1\over{\sqrt{2}}$ about its center. This can also be thought of as scaling it about its top-left point, then applying a transformation of ${{1\over 2}(n - {1\over \sqrt{2}}n)}$ in both dimensions. 

Most of the time you'll want a scale factor somewhere between $1$ and $1\over{\sqrt{2}}$. We can express this by substituting an arbitrary value $s$ for our scale factor, so side length becomes $sn$ and transformation becomes ${1\over 2}(n - sn)$. 

>[!TIP]
> The transformation equation ${1\over 2}(n - sn)$ may also be expressed as ${n(1-s)}\over 2$, which may be easier to calculate since we only use $n$ once. 
> 
> Note that due to floating point mathematics and the difference in order of operations, these may produce very subtly results answers on a computer or calculator, in the realm of $\pm2.0\times10^{-16}$.

## Choosing a Stitch Count

Next, we need to choose our stitch count. Stitch count is a little like DPI (dots per inch), in that it expresses how many stitches will fit into 1 inch of aida. A higher stitch count means a more detailed pattern, but is trickier to work with.

## Choosing a Hoop

Once we've chosen our thread count, which we will call $c$, we can find the diamter of the hoop very easily. 

We need a hoop that can fit the full diagonal of our pattern with our thread count. First we need the real side length of our pattern, which is our calculated side length $n$ divided by thread count $c$. This can be expressed as $w\text{ (in)} = {n\over c}$. To find the minimum diameter, we apply Pythagoras' theorem: $d_{min} \text{ (in)} = \sqrt{w^2 + w^2}$. 

## Measuring Thread

To stitch $n$ crosses, you need $4n-1$ lines (including the ones on the back of the aida). Each of these lines will measure ${1\over c}\text{ in}$, so for $n$ stitches of a given colour, you'll need ${1\over c}(4n-1)\text{ in}$ of thread (plus a little more compensate for tension and the start and end knots).
